The spinor and Weierstrass representations of surfaces in space
Ivan Solonenko

Abstract
. In this paper, following Sullivan ( [ 10 ] ) and Kusner and Schmitt ( [ 5 ] ), we study conformal immersions of Riemann surfaces into the three-dimensional Euclidean space. Regarding such immersions as special bundle maps from the tangent bundle of the surface to the cotangent bundle of the 2-dimensional sphere, we generalize the classical Weierstrass representation of minimal surfaces to the case of arbitrary conformal immersions. We study how such an immersion gives rise to a spin structure on the surface together with a pair of spinors and how the immersion itself can be studied by means of these spinors.



1.
Introduction

The Weierstrass representation of a minimal surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a classical method that allows one to describe the surface – and its differential-geometric properties like the mean and Gaussian curvatures and the Gauss map – by means of a pair of functions on an open subset of the complex plane, one of which is holomorphic and the other one is meromorphic. Such a representation is local and depends on the choice of a local holomorphic coordinate on the surface (also known as isothermal coordinates, especially in earlier literature on the subject).

In his 1989 paper
[
10
]
, D. Sullivan observed that the Weierstrass representation can be rendered coordinate-free and global by replacing the aforementioned pair of functions with a triple of holomorphic 1-forms on the surface. This triple can be thought of as a holomorphic bundle map from the tangent bundle of the surface to the cotangent bundle of the 2-sphere. The unique spin structure of
\mathbb{{S}}^{2}
can be then pulled back along this bundle map to induce a spin structure on the surface together with a pair of holomorphic sections of the corresponding line bundle of spinors. All of the original formulas describing the surface and its geometry can be rewritten neatly in terms of these two spinors. Conversely, given a Riemann surface with a fixed spin structure and a (nondegenerate) pair of holomorphic spinors, one can write down an explicit integral formula involving these spinors that gives a (possibly periodic) minimal immersion of the surface into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Later in their article
[
5
]
, R. Kusner and N. Schmitt observed that the minimality condition in Sullivan’s paper can be relinquished. The resulting ’generalized Weierstrass representation’ works for an arbitrary conformal immersion of a Riemann surface to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This way, holomorphic 1-forms and spinors become smooth
(1,0)
-forms and smooth spinors, respectively, and one has an added integrability condition that ensures that an abstract pair of spinors on
M
induces a (possibly periodic) conformal immersion of
M
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Both Sullivan’s and Kusner and Schmitt’s expositions of the subject are rather short on detail and barely contain any proofs. The present article is an attempt to rectify this issue. Following and improving on the papers of Sullivan, Kusner, and Schmitt, we give a detailed and thorough exposition of the generalized Weierstrass and spinor representations of Riemann surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we review the classical theory of Weierstrass representations of minimal surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In Section
3
we discuss a number of technical points related to
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, its canonical and tautological line bundles, and its Veronese embedding into
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
. In Section
4
we define (generalized) Weierstrass representations of a Riemann surface and study their relation to conformal immersions of the surface to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We also investigate how the geometric properties of a conformal immersion (for instance, whether it is minimal) can be expressed via its associated Weierstrass representation and how the latter can be used to recover the immersion itself. Finally, in Section
5
we introduce the notion of a spinor representation of a Riemann surface and show that it is essentially equivalent to that of a Weierstrass representation. We then reimagine the salient points of Section
4
in the language of spinor representations. As a side quest, we also give a formal categorical argument why a spin structure on a principal
\mathrm{SO}(2)
-bundle is the same as a so-called square root of the corresponding Hermitian line bundle.

2.
The classical Weierstrass representation

We start with a brief review of the classical theory of Weierstrass representations. Let
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an oriented minimal surface. By passing to local isothermal coordinates, we can think of
M
as the image of a conformal embedding
\upvarphi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
U
is an open subset of
\mathbb{C}
(we can actually allow
\upvarphi
to be an immersion, not necessarily an embedding). If we write
z=u+iv
, the conformality and minimality conditions on
\upvarphi
can be rewritten as
1
1

1

Throughout the paper,
\langle\hskip 1.00006pt{-}\hskip 1.00006pt|\hskip 1.00006pt{-}\hskip 1.00006pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
will stand for the standard bilinear form on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
:

•

(
\upvarphi
is a conformal)
||\upvarphi_{u}||=||\upvarphi_{v}||,\hskip 1.0pt\langle\hskip 1.00006pt{\upvarphi_{u}}\hskip 1.00006pt|\hskip 1.00006pt{\upvarphi_{v}}\hskip 1.00006pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0\hskip 5.0pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 5.0pt\langle\hskip 1.00006pt{\upvarphi_{z}}\hskip 1.00006pt|\hskip 1.00006pt{\upvarphi_{z}}\hskip 1.00006pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
,

•

(
\upvarphi
is minimal
\Leftrightarrow
harmonic)
\Updelta\hskip 0.7pt\upvarphi=0\;\Leftrightarrow\;\upvarphi_{z\bar{z}}=0
.

Now assume
U
is simply connected. Then there is a smooth map
\widehat{\upvarphi}\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfying

\left\{\begin{aligned} \widehat{\upvarphi}_{u}&=-\upvarphi_{v},\\
\widehat{\upvarphi}_{v}&=\upvarphi_{u},\end{aligned}\right.

and it is unique up to a translation in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. It is also a conformal minimal immersion (
adjoint
to
\upvarphi
). The conditions on
\widehat{\upvarphi}
are taken to be as they are precisely so that the immersion
f=\upvarphi+i\widehat{\upvarphi}\colon U\to\mathbb{C}^{3}
is holomorphic. It is also isotropic, meaning that
\langle\hskip 1.00006pt{f^{\prime}}\hskip 1.00006pt|\hskip 1.00006pt{f^{\prime}}\hskip 1.00006pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
. Conversely, given a holomorphic isotropic immersion of
U
to
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, its real and imaginary parts are adjoint conformal minimal immersions of
U
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. If we write
f^{\prime}=(\Upphi_{1},\Upphi_{2},\Upphi_{3})
, we have:

\Upphi_{1}^{2}+\Upphi_{2}^{2}+\Upphi_{3}^{2}=0.

This condition can be rewritten as

(2.1)

(\Upphi_{1}+i\Upphi_{2})(\Upphi_{1}-i\Upphi_{2})=-\Upphi_{3}^{2}.

Casting aside the cases when
\Upphi_{1}=i\Upphi_{2}
on the entire
U
(which is definitely not the case unless
\upvarphi
is an immersion into a horizontal plane), we can rewrite this as

\Upphi_{1}+i\Upphi_{2}=\frac{-\Upphi_{3}^{2}}{\Upphi_{1}-i\Upphi_{2}}.

We introduce a pair of functions on
U
:

\upmu=\Upphi_{1}-i\Upphi_{2},\quad\upnu=\frac{\Upphi_{3}}{\Upphi_{1}-i\Upphi_{2}}.

Clearly,
\upmu
is holomorphic and
\upnu
is meromorphic. Observe that the function
\upmu\upnu^{2}=-\Upphi_{1}-i\Upphi_{2}
is holomorphic. What is more, since
f
is an immersion, all
\Upphi_{i}
’s cannot vanish simultaneously at any point of
U
. Together with (
2.1
), this implies that if
p\in U
is a zero of
\upmu
, it cannot also be a zero of
\upmu\upnu^{2}
. We conclude that
\upmu
and
\upnu
are related by the following condition: the set of zeroes of
\upmu
coincides with the set of poles of
\upnu
, and given any point
p
in this set,
\mathrm{ord}_{p}(\upmu)=-2\mathrm{ord}_{p}(\upnu)
.

We can recover
f^{\prime}
using these two functions:

\Upphi_{1}=\frac{\upmu}{2}(1-\upnu^{2}),\quad\Upphi_{2}=\frac{i\upmu}{2}(1+\upnu^{2}),\quad\Upphi_{3}=\upmu\upnu.

The function
f^{\prime}
, in turn, determines
f
(and hence
\upvarphi
and
\widehat{\upvarphi}
) up to translations in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
by means of integration along paths in
U
:

f(p)=f(p_{0})+\int_{p_{0}}^{p}f^{\prime}dz,

where
p_{0}\in U
is fixed. This integral does not depend on the choice of a path because the 1-form
f^{\prime}dz=df
is exact. We see that, up to a translation in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the initial immersion
\upvarphi
can be expressed as the real part of an integral of some holomorphic 1-form (or rather a triple of those) written in terms of
\upmu
and
\upnu
:

(2.2)

\upvarphi(p)=\upvarphi(p_{0})+\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt\left(\int_{p_{0}}^{p}\frac{\upmu}{2}(1-\upnu^{2})dz,\;\int_{p_{0}}^{p}\frac{i\upmu}{2}(1+\upnu^{2})dz,\;\int_{p_{0}}^{p}\upmu\upnu dz\right).

Formula (
2.2
) is called the Weierstrass representation of
M
. Going in the opposite direction, one can start with a holomorphic function
\upmu
and a meromorphic function
\upnu
that are related by the aforementioned condition
2
2

2

One can actually relax this condition to the requirement that
\upmu\upnu^{2}
be holomorphic, but then one has to consider more general (so-called “branched”) minimal immersions:
\upvarphi
is required to be harmonic, nonconstant, and conformal outside of its singular points (which will automatically be discrete).
: the zeroes of
\upmu
are precisely the poles of
\upnu
and, given any such zero/pole
p\in U
,
\mathrm{ord}_{p}(\upmu)=-2\mathrm{ord}_{p}(\upnu)
. Using the same integral formula (
2.2
), one obtains a conformal minimal immersion
U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

We will render this theory coordinate-free and generalize it to arbitrary conformal immersions of Riemann surfaces into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Having done that, we will be able to reinterpret the theory completely in the language of spinors.

3.
The sphere, its canonical bundle, and the Veronese embedding

Before we proceed to main part, we need to discuss a number of technical points regarding the canonical bundle of the sphere and the Veronese embedding.

Agreement
.

Whenever we have a map between smooth manifolds, it is assumed to be smooth, unless mentioned otherwise. Also, we are going to meet a lot of interplay between real and complex geometry, so let us agree on the following. Whenever
E
is a complex vector space or complex vector bundle,
E^{*}
will stand for its
real
dual. Its complex dual will be denoted by
E^{*1,0}\subseteq E^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The two are surely isomorphic as complex vector spaces/bundles by means of taking the real part inside
E^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, where the complex structure on
E^{*}
is taken to be dual to the one on
E
. We often suppress this isomorphism from the notation. In case we want to stress whether the functionals being considered are real- or complex-valued, we will stick to this notation to avoid confusion. The conjugate
E^{*0,1}=\overline{\displaystyle E^{*1,0}}\subseteq E^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the antidual to
E
(the space/bundle of
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear functionals).

The first technical moment is how one identifies
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
. There is a bunch of similar ways to do this, all of them use the stereographic projection, but there is a degree of freedom in picking a pole of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
and an affine chart on
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
. We choose the stereographic projection from the North pole
N=(0,0,1)
:

Then we identify
\mathbb{C}
with the affine chart
U_{0}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{[z_{0}\colon z_{1}]\mid z_{0}\neq 0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}=\mathbb{C}P^{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{[0:1]}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}\subset\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and send
N
to the missing point
[0:1]
. The resulting map
\mathbb{S}^{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
is given by

(x,y,z)\mapsto\begin{cases}[1-z:x+iy],&\text{if}\hskip 3.99994ptz\neq 1,\\
[0:1],&\text{if}\hskip 3.99994ptz=1,\end{cases}

and it is a biholomorphism when the sphere is oriented by a vector field pointing
inside
the unit ball (this is
the opposite
of the boundary orientation of
\partial\mathbb{B}^{2}=\mathbb{S}^{2}
). The inverse mapping looks like this:

\begin{cases}[1:z]\mapsto\left(\dfrac{2\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007ptz}{|z|^{2}+1},\dfrac{2\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993ptz}{|z|^{2}+1},\dfrac{|z|^{2}-1}{|z|^{2}+1}\right),\\
[0:1]\mapsto N.\end{cases}

The projective line, in turn, can be identified with the standard conic in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
by means of the Veronese embedding:

\mathbb{C}P^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{2},\;[z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[z_{0}^{2}:z_{0}z_{1}:z_{1}^{2}].

The image of this map is cut out be the equation
w_{0}w_{2}=w_{1}^{2}
. It is a unique smooth plane conic up to projective automorphisms of
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
. It would be convenient for us to apply such an automorphism, i.e. linearly change the coordinates on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \widetilde{w}_{0}&=w_{0}-w_{2};\\
\widetilde{w}_{1}&=i(w_{0}+w_{2});\\
\widetilde{w}_{2}&=2w_{1}.\end{aligned}\right.

In these new coordinates, which we are going to denote
w_{0},w_{1},w_{2}
from now on, our conic becomes
[Q]=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{[w_{0}:w_{1}:w_{2}]\in\mathbb{C}P^{2}\mid w_{0}^{2}+w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}^{2}=0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. Let us denote the affine cone over it by
Q\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
. It can also be described as the set of isotropic vectors of the standard nondegenerate quadratic form on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. In these new coordinates, the Veronese embedding is given by:

\tilde{\uptheta}\colon\mathbb{C}P^{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}[Q]\subset\mathbb{C}P^{2},\;[z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[z_{0}^{2}-z_{1}^{2}:i(z_{0}^{2}+z_{1}^{2}):2z_{0}z_{1}].

This is the projectivization of the map

\uptheta\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{3},\;(z_{0},z_{1})\mapsto(z_{0}^{2}-z_{1}^{2},i(z_{0}^{2}+z_{1}^{2}),2z_{0}z_{1}).

The image of
\uptheta
is
Q
, and, when restricted to the set of nonzero vectors,
\uptheta
gives a two-sheeted holomorphic covering

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\uptheta}
\textstyle{Q}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{[Q]}(-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}P^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\uptheta}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{[Q]}

Now,
\uptheta
is a homogeneous polynomial map, so it sends (linear) lines in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
to lines in
Q
. This correctly defines a (nonlinear!) bundle map from
\upgamma\colon\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-1)\to\mathcal{O}_{[Q]}(-1)
covering
\tilde{\uptheta}
. As a map to
\mathbb{C}P^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}
,
\upgamma
is just the product of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-1)\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{1}\xrightarrow{\tilde{\uptheta}}[Q]
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-1)\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\xrightarrow{\uptheta}\mathbb{C}^{3}
, so it is holomorphic. Outside of the zero sections, it is just
\uptheta
composed and precomposed with two biholomorphisms as in the upper part of the diagram above (so it is also a two-sheeted holomorphic covering map). On the fibers,
\upgamma
looks like
\mathbb{C}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C},z\mapsto z^{2}
. But such a quadratic bundle map is the same as a linear bundle isomorphism (also covering
\tilde{\uptheta}
)
\widetilde{\upgamma}\colon\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-1)^{\otimes 2}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-2)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathcal{O}_{[Q]}(-1)
, which sends
v\otimes v
to
\upgamma(v)
. Since
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-2)
is isomorphic to the canonical bundle of
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, we arrive at the following:

Conclusion
.

There is a commutative square of isomorphisms of holomorphic line bundles over
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and
[Q]
:

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\upgamma}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

\sim

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{[Q]}(-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\sim

\textstyle{T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces T^{*}[Q]}
\scriptstyle{d\tilde{\uptheta}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

Both horizontal maps cover
\tilde{\uptheta}
.

It will also be vital for us to have an explicit description of the vertical isomorphisms in this diagram. It suffices to describe the left one. First we trivialize both
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-2)
and
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
over
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
with the same cocycle. We trivialize
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-1)
by means of the sections
\upsigma_{0}([z_{0}:z_{1}])=(1,\tfrac{z_{1}}{z_{0}})
and
\upsigma_{1}([z_{0}:z_{1}])=(\tfrac{z_{0}}{z_{1}},1)
, which automatically trivializes
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-2)
by
\upsigma_{0}\otimes\upsigma_{0}
and
\upsigma_{1}\otimes\upsigma_{1}
. For
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}\cong T^{*1,0}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, we take the section
-2d(\frac{z_{1}}{z_{0}})
and
2d(\frac{z_{0}}{z_{1}})
. One can readily check that the transition function for both of these trivializations is
\uptau_{01}\colon U_{0}\cap U_{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times},[z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto(\frac{z_{0}}{z_{1}})^{2}
. Thus, we can construct an isomorphism between our bundles just by sending the trivializing sections to each other:

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-2)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}T^{*1,0}\mathbb{C}P^{1},\;(z_{0},z_{1})\otimes(z^{\prime}_{0},z^{\prime}_{1})\mapsto\begin{cases}-2z_{0}z^{\prime}_{0}d(\frac{z_{1}}{z_{0}})_{[z_{0}:z_{1}]},&\text{if}\hskip 3.99994pt[z_{0}:z_{1}]\in U_{0};\\
2z_{1}z^{\prime}_{1}d(\frac{z_{0}}{z_{1}})_{[z_{0}:z_{1}]},&\text{if}\hskip 3.99994pt[z_{0}:z_{1}]\in U_{1},\end{cases}

where
(z_{0},z_{1})
and
(z^{\prime}_{0},z^{\prime}_{1})
are points on the line corresponding to
[z_{0}:z_{1}]
. Any other holomorphic isomorphism between these bundles differs from this one by a scalar because
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
is compact. We make this particular choice in order to get rid of some coefficients later on (see Proposition
13
).

4.
Conformal immersions and Weierstrass representations

In this section we define (generalized) Weierstrass representations of a Riemann surface and study their relation to conformal immersions of the surface into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We show that to every conformal immersion
M\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
one can associate a Weierstrass representation of
M
, and the latter can be used to study the geometry of the immersion and even recover the immersion itself.

4.1.
Weierstrass representations

We begin with the following general commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{SO}(2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{SO}(2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GL}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{U}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\sim

\textstyle{\mathrm{GL}(1,\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\sim

\textstyle{\mathbb{T}\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\sim

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\sim

The right column tells us that if
V
is a two-dimensional real vector space, to pick a complex structure on it (a reduction of the frame space
\mathrm{F}(V)
from
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
to
\mathrm{GL}(1,\mathbb{C})
) is the same as to pick an orientation and a conformal class of Euclidean inner products (a reduction to
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{SO}(2)
). Assume such a datum is chosen and fixed and let
I
denote the complex structure. Then, for any
v\in V
,
v
and
Iv
are of the same length and they form an oriented orthogonal basis for
V
. In particular,
I
is orthogonal with respect to any inner product within the given conformal class. The left column tells us that to choose a particular inner product within our class is the same as to choose a Hermitian inner product on the one-dimensional complex vector space
(V,I)
– both are further reductions of
\mathrm{F}(V)
to
\mathrm{SO}(2)\cong\mathrm{U}(1)
. As an
(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{SO}(2))
-set (resp.,
\mathrm{GL}(1,\mathbb{C})
-set), the reduction
\mathrm{F}_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{SO}(2)}(V)
(resp.,
\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{GL}(1,\mathbb{C})}(V)
) is isomorphic to
V\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. In terms of the corresponding Euclidean and Hermitian inner products, this orbit is the circle of unit-length vectors. Finally, these Hermitian and Euclidean products
h
and
g
come from the same Kähler triple:
h(v,w)=g(v,w)+i\upomega(v,w)
, where
\upomega(v,w)=g(v,Iw)
is the fundamental symplectic form of
g
and
I
(and also the volume form of
g
).

The discussion above applies almost verbatim when
V
is replaced with a rank-2 real vector bundle
L
over a smooth manifold
M
. The frame bundle
\mathrm{F}(L)
is a principal
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-bundle over
M
, its reduction to
\mathrm{GL}(1,\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{SO}(2)
is the same as a choice of either an almost complex structure in
L
or an orientation together with a conformal class of Euclidean bundle metrics (we will occasionally call this a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-reduction of
\mathrm{F}(L)
or a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-structure in
L
). Its further reduction to
\mathrm{SO}(2)\cong\mathrm{U}(1)
is a choice of a particular metric within the given conformal class or of a Hermitian bundle metric in
L
thought of as a complex line bundle (a
\mathbb{T}
-reduction of
\mathrm{F}(L)
or a
\mathbb{T}
-structure in
L
). This interplay between almost complex structures, bundle metrics, and orientations will pop up numerous times throughout the paper so it is worthwhile to have it laid out at the beginning.

The special case of prime interest for us is when
M
is two-dimensional and
L=TM
. When a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-reduction of
\mathrm{F}(TM)
is specified,
M
is a Riemann surface
3
3

3

This is because every almost complex structure on a 2-dimensional manifold is integrable by the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem.
. Note that in this case, as a complex line bundle,
TM
is isomorphic to
T^{1,0}M
by means of projection onto the latter along
T^{0,1}M
inside
T_{\mathbb{C}}M
.

Before we define Weierstrass representations, we need one more observation that will prove useful throughout the paper.

Observation
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold and
E
a complex vector bundle over
M
. A section of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(E,M\times\mathbb{C}^{m})\cong E^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}^{m}\cong(E^{*}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\oplus m}
is the same as a smooth map
E\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
that is
\mathbb{R}
-linear on each fiber. Such a section lies in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(E,M\times\mathbb{C}^{m})\cong E^{*1,0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}^{m}\cong(E^{*1,0})^{\oplus m}
if and only if it is
\mathbb{C}
-linear on each fiber as a map
E\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
. If, in addition,
M
is a complex manifold and
E
is a holomorphic vector bundle, such a section is holomorphic if and only if it is holomorphic as a map
E\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
. A special case of this is when
M
is a complex manifold and
E=TM
. A
\mathbb{C}^{m}
-valued 1-form on
M
(an
m
-tuple of complex-valued 1-forms) is the same as a smooth map
TM\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
that is
\mathbb{R}
-linear on each fiber. Such a form is of type (1,0) if and only if it is
\mathbb{C}
-linear on each fiber as a map
TM\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
, and holomorphic if and only if it is holomorphic as a map
TM\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

Now we can finally proceed to defining what a Weierstrass representation is. It is going to be an object that can be assigned to an arbitrary Riemann surface
M
as a piece of extra data. Moreover, any conformal immersion
M\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is going to determine such an object in a unique and well-defined way. This latter construction will serve as a motivation for the definition of a Weierstrass representation, so this is where we begin.

Let
M
be any Riemann surface. Let
\upvarphi=(X,Y,Z)\colon M\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth map. Then
\boldsymbol{\upomega}=2\partial\upvarphi=d\upvarphi-iI^{*}d\upvarphi
is a smooth
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued (1,0)-form (just a triple of (1,0)-forms) on
M
(here
\partial
is the (1,0)-part of the de Rham differential
d
on
M
). Alternatively,
\upomega
can be described as
TM\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}T^{1,0}M\xrightarrow{2d\upvarphi}\mathbb{C}^{3}
:
\upomega(v)=d\upvarphi(v)-id\upvarphi(Iv)
. In a local holomorphic coordinate
z
,
\upomega=2\upvarphi_{z}dz
, where
\upvarphi_{z}=\frac{\partial\upvarphi}{\partial z}
. Since we have a fixed conformal class of Riemannian metrics on
M
and the standard Riemannian metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, it makes sense, for any
p\in M
, to ask whether
d\upvarphi_{p}\colon T_{p}M\to T_{\upvarphi(p)}\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a conformal map. If this is the case for all the points where the differential
d\upvarphi
does not vanish, we call
\upvarphi

pseudoconformal
. Note that this condition implies that
\upvarphi
is automatically an immersion at all such points.

Lemma 1
.

Let
M
be a Riemann surface and
\upvarphi\colon M\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
a smooth map.

(1)

\upvarphi
is pseudoconformal if and only if the image of
TM
under
\upomega
lies in
Q\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

(2)

\upvarphi
is a conformal immersion if and only if
\upomega
does not vanish at any point and the image of
TM
under
\upomega
lies in
Q
.

Proof.

A straightforward computation. Let us write the components of
\upomega
as
\upomega_{i}
. Then, given
p\in M
and
v\in T_{p}M
nonzero, we have

\displaystyle\upomega_{1}(v)^{2}+

\displaystyle\upomega_{2}(v)^{2}+\upomega_{3}(v)^{2}=

\displaystyle=(dX(v)-idX(Iv))^{2}+(dY(v)-idY(Iv))^{2}+(dZ(v)-idZ(Iv))^{2}

\displaystyle=||d\upvarphi(v)||^{2}-2i\langle\hskip 1.00006pt{d\upvarphi(v)}\hskip 1.00006pt|\hskip 1.00006pt{d\upvarphi(Iv)}\hskip 1.00006pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt-||d\upvarphi(Iv)||^{2}.

But then

\displaystyle\upomega(v)\in Q\;

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;\upomega_{1}(v)^{2}+\upomega_{2}(v)^{2}+\upomega_{3}(v)^{2}=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &||d\upvarphi(v)||=||d\upvarphi(Iv)||\\
&d\upvarphi(v)\perp d\upvarphi(Iv)\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;\text{$\upvarphi$ is conformal at $p$ or $d\upvarphi$ vanishes at $p$.}

This proves (1). For (2), just note that
\upvarphi
is a conformal immersion
\Leftrightarrow

\upvarphi
is pseudoconformal and
d\upvarphi
does not vanish at any point
\Leftrightarrow
the image of
TM
under
\upomega
lies in
Q
and
\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(\upomega)
does not vanish. But the zeroes of a
(1,0)
-form coincide with the zeroes of its real part, for
\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt(\upomega)=-I^{*}\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(\upomega)
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 2
.

The same remains true for maps
M\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
for any
n
when
Q
is defined to be the cone of isotropic vectors of the standard nondegenerate quadratic form on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

As a consequence of Lemma
1
, the pseudoconformality condition on
\upvarphi
can be rewritten as an equation on
\upomega_{i}
’s in the space on quadratic differentials on
M
:

(4.1)

\upomega_{1}^{2}+\upomega_{2}^{2}+\upomega_{3}^{2}=0.

Observation
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold,
E
a complex line bundle over
M
,
[P]\subseteq\mathbb{C}P^{n}
a complex submanifold,
P\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
the affine cone over
[P]
, and, finally,
\mathcal{O}_{[P]}(-1)
the tautological line bundle over
[P]
. Assume we have a smooth
\mathbb{C}
-linear bundle map from
E\to M
to
\mathcal{O}_{[P]}(-1)\to[P]
that is an isomorphism on each fiber (we will call such bundle maps nonvanishing). Then, projecting
\mathcal{O}_{[P]}(-1)\subseteq[P]\times\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
onto
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
, we obtain a smooth map
E\to\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
that is
\mathbb{C}
-linear and nonzero on every fiber and whose image lies in
P
(in other words, it maps every fiber
\mathbb{C}
-linearly onto a line in
P
). But this is the same as a smooth nonvanishing section of
(E^{*1,0})^{\oplus(n+1)}
mapping
E
to
P
. Conversely, given such a section
\upomega\colon E\to\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
, we have

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

The left vertical arrow is a smooth surjective submersion (as is the right one, for that matter), so
\uppi_{P}\circ\upomega
passes through it to give a smooth map
\upeta\colon M\to[P]
, and
(\upeta\circ\uppi_{E},\upomega)\colon E\to\mathcal{O}_{[P]}(-1)
is a smooth nonvanishing
\mathbb{C}
-linear bundle map covering
\upeta
. In particular, when
[P]=\mathbb{C}P^{n}
, nonvanishing
\mathbb{C}
-linear bundle maps from
E\to M
to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{n}}(-1)\to\mathbb{C}P^{n}
correspond simply to nonvanishing sections of
(E^{*1,0})^{\oplus(n+1)}
. If
M
is a complex manifold and
E
is a holomorphic vector bundle, such a bundle map is holomorphic if and only if the corresponding section of
(E^{*1,0})^{\oplus(n+1)}
is holomorphic (by the observation on page
Observation
and the fact that
\uppi_{E}
is a holomorphic surjective submersion).

With regard to Riemann surfaces, this gives us the following

Corollary 3
.

Let
M
be a Riemann surface. Then one has the following bijections:

\resizebox{21931650}{}{
$\left\{\begin{gathered}\text{nonvanishing triples}\\
(\upomega_{i})\;\text{of (1,0)-forms}\\
\text{s.\hskip 1.0ptth.}\;\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{3}{\upomega_{i}^{2}}=\hskip 1.00006pt0\\
\end{gathered}\right\}\rightleftarrows\left\{\begin{gathered}\text{maps}\;TM\xrightarrow{\upomega}\mathbb{C}^{3}\;\text{that}\\
\text{map each fiber $\mathbb{C}$-linearly}\\
\text{onto a line in $Q$}\\
\end{gathered}\right\}\rightleftarrows\left\{\begin{gathered}\text{nonvanishing $\mathbb{C}$-linear bundle}\\
\text{maps}\;(\upomega,\upeta)\;\text{from}\;TM\to M\\
\text{to}\;\mathcal{O}_{[Q]}(-1)\to[Q]\\
\end{gathered}\right\}$
}

And as we already know, the latter are the same as nonvanishing bundle maps to
(T^{*}[Q]\to[Q])\cong(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to\mathbb{C}P^{1})\cong(T^{*}\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}^{2})
.

Definition 4
.

Given a Riemann surface
M
, we call an element of any of the three sets in Corollary
3
a
Weierstrass representation of

\boldsymbol{M}
.

Conclusion
.

Lemma
1
simply says that any conformal immersion of
M
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
induces a Weierstrass representation of
M
.

4.2.
The Gauss map

We are going to investigate how a conformal immersion
\upvarphi\colon M\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and its geometrical properties can be studied by means of the corresponding Weierstrass representation
\upomega
. In this subsection we give a geometric interpretation to the map
\upeta\colon M\to[Q]
induced by
\upomega
.

First of all, we establish some notation that will facilitate a lot of proofs in the sequel. Let
M
be a Riemann surface, and let
\upomega
be any Weierstrass representation of
M
. In a similar vein to what we did in Section
2
, let us rewrite the quadratic equation (
4.1
) as:

(4.2)

(\upomega_{1}+i\upomega_{2})(\upomega_{1}-i\upomega_{2})=-\upomega_{3}^{2}.

The idea is that, since
\upomega_{1},\upomega_{2}
, and
\upomega_{3}
are related by an equation, some data are redundant and can be dispensed with. Denote
\boldsymbol{\upmu_{+}}=\upomega_{1}+i\upomega_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\upmu_{-}}=\upomega_{1}-i\upomega_{2}
. Let
\boldsymbol{M_{+}},\boldsymbol{M_{-}}\subseteq M
be their respective zero loci. Since
\upomega
is nowhere-vanishing, these are disjoint closed sets, and their union is exactly the zero locus of
\upomega_{3}
. Let us denote the complements of
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
by
\boldsymbol{U_{+}}
and
\boldsymbol{U_{-}}
, respectively. The
(1,0)
-form
\upmu_{\pm}
is a nowhere-vanishing smooth section of the complex line bundle
T^{*1,0}M
over
U_{\pm}
. Therefore, there exists a unique smooth complex-valued function
\boldsymbol{\upnu_{\pm}}
on
U_{\pm}
such that
\upnu_{\pm}\upmu_{\pm}=\upomega_{3}
(slightly informally,
\upnu_{\pm}=\frac{\upomega_{3}}{\upmu_{\pm}}
). Together,
\upmu_{\pm}
and
\upnu_{\pm}
recover
\upomega
over
U_{\pm}
:

(4.3)

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\upomega\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U_{\pm}}}=\left(\frac{1-\upnu_{\pm}^{2}}{2},\pm\frac{i(1+\upnu_{\pm}^{2})}{2},\upnu_{\pm}\right)\upmu_{\pm}.

We also have the following relations:

\upmu_{+}\upmu_{-}=-\upomega_{3}^{2};\quad\upnu_{+}\upnu_{-}=-1\;\text{(over $U_{+}\cap U_{-}$)};\quad\nu_{\pm}^{-1}(0)=M_{\mp}.

Both pairs
(\upmu_{+},\upnu_{+})
and
(\upmu_{-},\upnu_{-})
are equally useful and none of them can recover the Weierstrass representation in general, for the set
M_{\pm}
of “poles” (see p.
2
) of
\upnu_{\pm}
can be arbitrarily large. If either
M_{+}
or
M_{-}
has empty interior, (
4.3
) recovers
\upomega
entirely by continuity. For instance, as we will see in Proposition
11
, this is the case when
\upomega
comes from a minimal immersion.

Agreement
.

For consistency, we will mostly use the pair
(\upmu_{-},\upnu_{-})
, so in the proofs we will write it as
(\boldsymbol{\upmu},\boldsymbol{\upnu})
to simplify the notation.

For every
p\in M
,
d\upvarphi(T_{p}M)
is an oriented plane in
T_{\upvarphi(p)}\mathbb{R}^{3}\cong\mathbb{R}^{3}
, so the normal line
d\upvarphi(T_{p}M)^{\perp}
is naturally oriented as well (in such a way that the orientations in the decomposition
T_{\upvarphi(p)}\mathbb{R}^{3}=d\upvarphi(T_{p}M)\oplus d\upvarphi(T_{p}M)^{\perp}
match). This allows us to choose a positively oriented unit section
4
4

4

Which is a unit normal vector field along
\upvarphi(M)
in case
\upvarphi
is an embedding (hence always locally).
of
TM^{\perp}\subseteq\upvarphi^{*}T\mathbb{R}^{3}\cong M\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which can be thought of as a smooth map
M\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
. It is called the
Gauss map of

\boldsymbol{\upvarphi}
. Using the notation established above, we are ready to prove the following important

Proposition 5
.

Let
\upvarphi
be a conformal immersion of
M
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
(\upomega,\upeta)
its Weierstrass representation. Under the identifications
\mathbb{S}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}P^{1}\cong[Q]
,
\upeta\colon M\to[Q]
is exactly the Gauss map of
\upvarphi
.

Proof.

Step 1:

The Gauss map and
\upeta
agree on
M_{-}
.
The North pole
N
of the sphere goes to
[0:1]
in
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and hence to
[-1:i:0]=[i:1:0]
in
[Q]
under our identifications. But the preimage of this point by
\upeta
in exactly
M_{-}
. So we need to show that the Gauss image of a point is
N
if and only if it lies in
M_{-}
. Let
p\in M
and
v\in T_{p}M\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -0.69 6.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. We compute:

p\in M_{-}\;\Leftrightarrow\;\upmu(p)=0\;\Leftrightarrow\;\upomega_{1}(v)=i\upomega_{2}(v)\;\Leftrightarrow\;\left\{\begin{aligned} &dX(Iv)=-dY(v);\\
&dY(Iv)=dX(v);\\
&dZ(v)=dZ(Iv)=0\end{aligned}\;\Leftrightarrow\right.\\[5.0pt]
\Leftrightarrow\;\begin{aligned} &d\upvarphi(Iv)\;\text{can be obtained from}\;d\upvarphi(v)\;\text{by the}\\
&\text{clockwise rotation around the z-axis by $90^{\circ}$}\end{aligned}\;\Leftrightarrow\;\text{the Gauss image of $p$ is $N$.}

Step 2:

The Gauss map and
\upeta
agree on
U_{-}
.
It is easy to see that, as a map to
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\upeta\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U_{-}}}
is just
[1:\upnu]
. Therefore, as a map to
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, it is
\left(\frac{2\hskip 0.57367pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.57367pt\upnu}{|\upnu|^{2}+1},\frac{2\hskip 0.65556pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.32771pt\upnu}{|\upnu|^{2}+1},\frac{|\upnu|^{2}-1}{|\upnu|^{2}+1}\right)
. So we need to show that the Gauss map is given by this formula over
U_{-}
. Pick any
p\in U_{-}
and nonzero
v\in T_{p}M
. First of all, we express the lengths of
d\upvarphi(v)
and
d\upvarphi(Iv)
via
\upnu
and
\upmu
:

\displaystyle||d

\displaystyle\upvarphi(v)||^{2}=||d\upvarphi(Iv)||^{2}=\frac{1}{2}||\upomega(v)||^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}|\upomega_{i}(v)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{(1-\upnu(p)^{2})(1-\overline{\upnu}(p)^{2})}{4}|\upmu(v)|^{2}+\frac{(1+\upnu(p)^{2})(1+\overline{\upnu}(p)^{2})}{4}|\upmu(v)|^{2}+|\upnu(p)|^{2}|\upmu(v)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\upmu(v)|^{2}}{8}\left(1-\upnu(p)^{2}-\overline{\upnu}(p)^{2}+|\upnu(p)|^{4}+1+\upnu(p)^{2}+\overline{\upnu}(p)^{2}+|\upnu(p)|^{4}+4|\upnu(p)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\upmu(v)|^{2}}{8}\left(2+4|\upnu(p)|^{2}+2|\upnu(p)|^{4}\right)=\left(\frac{|\upmu(v)|(1+|\upnu(p)|^{2})}{2}\right)^{2}.

Next, the cross-product of
d\upvarphi(v)
and
d\upvarphi(Iv)
is

d\upvarphi(v)\times d\upvarphi(Iv)=\left(\begin{gathered}dY(v)dZ(Iv)-dY(Iv)dZ(v)\\
-dX(v)dZ(Iv)+dX(Iv)dZ(v)\\
dX(v)dY(Iv)-dX(Iv)dY(v)\end{gathered}\right)=\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt\left(\begin{gathered}\upomega_{2}(v)\hskip 0.39993pt\overline{\upomega_{3}(v)}\\
\overline{\upomega_{1}(v)}\hskip 0.39993pt\upomega_{3}(v)\\
\upomega_{1}(v)\hskip 0.39993pt\overline{\upomega_{2}(v)}\end{gathered}\right)\\[3.00003pt]
=\frac{|\upmu(v)|^{2}}{4}\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt\left(\begin{gathered}2i(1+\upnu(p)^{2})\overline{\upnu(p)}\\
2(1-\overline{\upnu(p)}^{2})\upnu(p)\\
i(\upnu(p)^{2}-1)(\overline{\upnu(p)^{2}}+1)\end{gathered}\right)=\frac{|\upmu(v)|^{2}}{4}\left(\begin{gathered}2\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(\overline{\upnu(p)}+\upnu(p)^{2}\overline{\upnu(p)})\\
2\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt(\upnu(p)-\overline{\upnu(p)}^{2}\upnu(p))\\
\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(|\upnu(p)|^{4}+\upnu(p)^{2}-\overline{\upnu(p)}^{2}-1)\end{gathered}\right)\\[3.00003pt]
=\frac{|\upmu(v)|^{2}}{4}\left(\begin{gathered}2\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt\upnu(p)(1+|\upnu(p)|^{2})\\
2\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt\upnu(p)(1+|\upnu(p)|^{2})\\
|\upnu(p)|^{4}-1\end{gathered}\right)=\frac{|\upmu(v)|^{2}(1+|\upnu(p)|^{2})}{4}\left(\begin{gathered}2\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt\upnu(p)\\
2\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt\upnu(p)\\
|\upnu(p)|^{2}-1\end{gathered}\right).

Dividing this by
||d\upvarphi(v)\times d\upvarphi(Iv)||=||d\upvarphi(v)||^{2}
yields the Gauss image of
p
:

\left(\frac{2\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt\upnu(p)}{|\upnu(p)|^{2}+1},\frac{2\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt\upnu(p)}{|\upnu(p)|^{2}+1},\frac{|\upnu(p)|^{2}-1}{|\upnu(p)|^{2}+1}\right),

which agrees with
\upeta(p)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Agreement
.

Given any Weierstrass representation
(\upomega,\upeta)
of
M
, when thinking of
\upeta
as a map to
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
or
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we will use the same letter. In particular, the Gauss map of a conformal immersion
M\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
will be denoted by
\upeta
from now on.

Remark 6
.

Given a Weierstrass representation
\upomega
of
M
,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\upeta\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U_{-}}}\colon U_{-}\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
is given simply by
[1:\upnu_{-}]
. This means that we can extend
\upnu_{-}
to the whole
M
by looking at it as a function
U_{-}\to\mathbb{C}\cong U_{0}\subset\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and defining it to be
[0:1]
on
M_{-}
(extending to the “poles” by “infinity” – just as we do for meromorphic functions; cf. p.
2
). The resulting map
M\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
is smooth, for it is just another manifestation of
\upeta
. In a similar vein, we can extend
\upnu_{+}
to the whole
M
by sending
M_{+}
to the “infinity”
[0:1]
. The resulting map
M\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
is also smooth, but it differs from
\upeta
by the involution
R=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1\;\;&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\in\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{C})
of
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
because
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\upeta\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U_{+}}}=[-\upnu_{+}:1]
. In terms of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
R
is the reflection in the y-axis.

4.3.
The minimal case

Now we turn our attention to the question of minimality. First, we need a couple of standard facts on harmonicity and the Hodge star operator:

Fact 7
.

Let
(M,g)
be an oriented Riemannian
n
-manifold, and let
f
be a smooth positive function on
M
. Then the Hodge star operators
\Upomega^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\Upomega^{n-k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and codifferentials
\Upomega^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)\to\Upomega^{k-1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
of the metrics
g
and
fg
are related as follows:

•

\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt_{fg}=f^{k-\frac{n}{2}}\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt_{g}
.

•

d^{*}_{fg}=\frac{1}{f}d^{*}_{g}+\frac{(-1)^{n(k+1)+1}(k-\frac{n}{2})}{f}\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt_{g}(df\wedge\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt_{g}(-))
.

Corollary 7.1
.

Let
M
be a Riemann surface. Pick any Riemannian metric within its conformal class.

(1)

The Hodge star operator
\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt
is a conformally invariant automorphism of
\Upomega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
, i.e. it does not depend on the choice of a metric within the conformal class (when restricted to 1-forms only). In fact, it is easy to see by taking orthonormal oriented frames that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\Upomega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)}}
coincides with
-I^{*}
.

(2)

A conformal factor
f
changes the codifferential
d^{*}_{g}\colon\Upomega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)\to C^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
(and hence the Laplacian
\Updelta_{g}=d^{*}_{g}\circ d
on
C^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
) by a factor of
\frac{1}{f}
. Consequently, the notion of harmonicity is a conformal invariant for smooth functions on
M
. In fact,
f
is harmonic if and only if both
\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007ptf
and
\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993ptf
are harmonic, and a real function is harmonic if and only if it is locally the real (or imaginary) part of some holomorphic function on
M
.

It will be useful for us to look at the notions of harmonicity of holomorphicity for functions and 1-forms on Riemann surfaces in slightly more detail. It will pay off further on and render some statements almost obvious. As always, let
M
be a Riemann surface.

(1)

The notion of coclosedness and hence harmonicity for 1-forms on
M
is also a conformal invariant. Indeed,
d^{*}_{fg}\upeta=\frac{1}{f}d^{*}_{g}\upeta=0\Leftrightarrow d^{*}_{g}\upeta=0
. A 1-form
\upeta
is coclosed if and only if
\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt\upeta
is closed.

(2)

A function
f
is harmonic if and only if
df
is harmonic. Therefore, a 1-form is harmonic if and only if it is locally the differential of a harmonic function.

(3)

The projection
\prod^{1,0}\colon\Upomega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)\to\Upomega^{1,0}(M)
sends
\upeta
to
\frac{1}{2}(\upeta+i\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt\upeta)
. Similarly,
\prod^{0,1}\upeta=\frac{1}{2}(\upeta-i\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt\upeta)
. Consequently, if
f
is a function,
\partial f=\frac{1}{2}(df+i\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003ptdf),\overline{\partial}f=\frac{1}{2}(df-i\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003ptdf)
. All of this follows from Corollary
7.1
(1).

(4)

A 1-form
\upeta
is of type (1,0) (resp., (0,1)) if and only if
\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt(\upeta)=\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(\upeta)
(resp.,
\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt(\upeta)=-\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(\upeta)
).

(5)

A function
f
is holomorphic if and only if
\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt(df)=\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(df)
.

(6)

Let
\upeta
be a (1,0)- or (0,1)-form. The following are equivalent:

(a)

\upeta
is closed.

(b)

\upeta
is coclosed.

(c)

\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(\upeta)
is harmonic.

(d)

\hskip 0.80002pt\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.39993pt(\upeta)
is harmonic.

(e)

\upeta
is harmonic.

This is a simple consequence of the previously observed fact that
\upeta
is closed if and only if
\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003pt\upeta
is coclosed.

(7)

(1,0)-forms satisfying conditions (i)-(v) above are precisely holomorphic 1-forms, since
d=\partial
on (1,0)-forms.

Now we are ready to prove

Proposition 8
.

Let
M
be a Riemann surface,
\upvarphi\colon M\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
a conformal immersion, and
\upomega=2\partial\upvarphi\in\Upomega^{1,0}(M,\mathbb{C}^{3})
its Weierstrass representation. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\upvarphi
is minimal;

(2)

\upvarphi
is harmonic;

(3)

\upomega
is holomorphic;

(4)

The bundle map
(\upomega,\upeta)
from
TM\to M
to
\mathcal{O}_{[Q]}(-1)\to[Q]
is holomorphic;

(5)

The Gauss map
\upeta\colon M\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
of
\upvarphi
is holomorphic.

Proof.

It is a standard fact that a submanifold of a Riemannian manifold is minimal if and only if its embedding into the ambient manifold is harmonic (see, e.g.,
[
11
]
). Since both properties are local, it remains true for isometric immersions, and since harmonicity is a conformal notion for surfaces, it remains true for conformal immersions of surfaces, so we have
(i)\Leftrightarrow(ii)
. But
\upomega=d\upvarphi+i\hskip 0.39993pt{\ast}\hskip 0.50003ptd\upvarphi
is a (1,0)-form, so
\upomega
is holomorphic
\Leftrightarrow\hskip 0.70007pt\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.70007pt(\upomega)=d\upvarphi
is harmonic
\Leftrightarrow\upvarphi
is harmonic, which gives
(ii)\Leftrightarrow(iii)
. Next,
(iii)\Leftrightarrow(iv)\Rightarrow(v)
follows from the observation on page
Observation
. For the remaining part
(v)\Rightarrow(ii)
we refer to
[
4
, Prop. 1.2.4]
.
∎

Remark 9
.

If
\upomega
is an arbitrary Weierstrass representation of
M
, not necessarily coming from a conformal immersion, then part
(iii)\Leftrightarrow(iv)\Rightarrow(v)
of Proposition
8
still remains true (with the same proof). We call a
Weierstrass representation
satisfying
(iii)-(iv)

holomorphic
. Observe that holomorphicity of
\upeta
implies holomorphicity of
\upomega
only when they come from a conformal immersion. As an example, take some
M
with a holomorphic Weierstrass representation
(\upomega,\upeta)
and multiply
\upomega
by a nonvanishing complex-valued smooth function
f
that is not holomorphic. The resulting Weierstrass representation
(f\upomega,\upeta)
is not holomorphic, although
\upeta
is.

Remark 10
.

It is essential in part
(iv)
of Proposition
8
that the orientation on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is chosen the way we did in Section
3
. If it was the opposite of ours, the Gauss map of a minimal immersion would never be holomorphic and, on the other hand, the Gauss map of a conformal immersion whose image lies in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
(and that is definitely not minimal) would be holomorphic.

Let
\upomega
be a holomorphic Weierstrass representation of
M
. Then
\upmu_{+}
and
\upmu_{-}
are holomorphic 1-forms on
M
and
\upnu_{+}
and
\upnu_{-}
are holomorphic functions on
U_{+}
and
U_{-}
, respectively. There are three distinct cases:

(1)

\upomega_{3}=0
(if
\upomega
comes from a conformal immersion
\upvarphi
, this is equivalent to
\upvarphi
being an immersion to a horizontal plane). This can be divided into:

(a)

\upmu_{+}=0
. Then
M_{+}=U_{-}=M,M_{-}=U_{+}=\varnothing
,
\upmu_{-}
does not vanish, and
\upnu_{-}=0
. If
\upomega
comes from a conformal immersion
\upvarphi
, this is equivalent to
\upvarphi
being an immersion to a horizontal plane with the Gauss map always pointing downward (
\upeta(M)=S
).

(b)

\upmu_{-}=0
. Then
M_{+}=U_{-}=\varnothing,M_{-}=U_{+}=M
,
\upmu_{+}
does not vanish, and
\upnu_{+}=0
. If
\upomega
comes from a conformal immersion
\upvarphi
, this is equivalent to
\upvarphi
being an immersion to a horizontal plane with the Gauss map always pointing upward (
\upeta(M)=N
).

(2)

\upomega_{3}\neq 0
. Then the set
M_{+}\sqcup M_{-}
of its zeroes is discrete. A priori,
\upnu_{\pm}
is a holomorphic function on
U_{\pm}
, but we have already seen in Remark
6
that it tends to infinity at the points of
M_{\pm}
, so it is actually a meromorphic function on
M
(given by the same formula
\frac{\omega_{3}}{\mu_{\pm}}
), and
M_{\pm}
is indeed the set of its poles.

Proposition 11
.

Let
M
be a Riemann surface. The map

\left\{\begin{gathered}\text{Weierstrass}\\
\text{representations of $M$}\end{gathered}\right\}\longrightarrow\left\{\begin{gathered}\text{pairs $(\upmu_{-},\upnu_{-})$ with $\upmu_{-}\in\Upomega^{1,0}(M)$}\\
\text{and $\upnu_{-}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}(M\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
